=== s3-access-point-definition | capability | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1,1.3.S6
C: An access point is a named network endpoint with its own access policy, attached to a data source such as a general purpose bucket, that simplifies managing access at scale to shared datasets.
Q: What is an S3 access point, and what problem does it solve?
V: definition=named network endpoints with dedicated access policies || purpose=simplify managing data access at scale for shared datasets
S: scope='Amazon S3 access points' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Welcome] Amazon S3 access points are named network endpoints with dedicated access policies that describe how data can be accessed using that endpoint. Access points are attached to an underlying data source, such as a general purpose bucket, directory bucket, or a FSx for OpenZFS volume, that you can use to perform S3 object operations, such as `GetObject` and `PutObject`. Access points simplify managing data access at scale for shared datasets in Amazon S3.
=== s3-access-point-needs-bucket-permission-too | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1,1.3.S6
C: To access objects through an access point, both the access point policy and the underlying bucket must permit the request; AWS recommends delegating access control from the bucket to its access points for buckets that need no direct access.
Q: Is an access point policy enough on its own, or must the bucket policy also allow the request?
V: v1=both the access point and the underlying bucket || v2=delegate access control for a bucket
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-points-policies] For an application or user to be able to access objects through an access point, both the access point and the underlying bucket or Amazon FSx file system must permit the request.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-points-policies] You can delegate access control for a bucket to the bucket's access points. The following example bucket policy allows full access to all access points that are owned by the bucket owner's account. Thus, all access to this bucket is controlled by the policies attached to its access points. We recommend configuring your buckets this way for all use cases that don't require direct access to the bucket.
=== s3-access-point-network-origin-vpc | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1,1.3.S6
C: An access point has a network origin of Internet or VPC. With network origin VPC, S3 rejects every request that does not come from the named VPC; the network origin is set at creation and cannot be changed afterwards.
Q: What does an access point with network origin VPC block, and can the origin be changed later?
V: reject=Amazon S3 rejects any request made to the access point that doesn't originate from that VPC || fixed=after you create the access point, you can't change its network origin || origins=network origin of Internet
S: scope='per access point' dom='fixed' adj=False cond='set with VpcConfiguration at creation'
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-points-vpc] An access point that's only accessible from a specified VPC has a network origin of `VPC`, and Amazon S3 rejects any request made to the access point that doesn't originate from that VPC.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-points-vpc] You can only specify an access point's network origin when you create the access point. After you create the access point, you can't change its network origin.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-points-vpc] An access point that's accessible from the internet is said to have a network origin of `Internet`.
=== s3-access-point-policy-like-bucket-policy | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S6
C: An access point policy is written like a bucket policy with the access point ARN as the resource. S3 evaluates all relevant policies (user, bucket, access point, VPC endpoint, SCPs and ACLs) when it authorizes a request through an access point.
Q: How is a request through an access point authorized?
V: like=you write access point policies like you would a bucket policy, using the access point ARN as the resource || all=evaluates all the relevant policies, including those on the user, bucket, access point, VPC Endpoint, and service control policies as well as Access Control Lists
S: scope='per access point' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 access points have their own IAM access point policy. You write access point policies like you would a bucket policy, using the access point ARN as the resource. Access point policies can grant or restrict access to the S3 data requested through the access point. Amazon S3 evaluates all the relevant policies, including those on the user, bucket, access point, VPC Endpoint, and service control policies as well as Access Control Lists, to decide whether to authorize the request.
=== s3-access-point-policy-only-through-access-point | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1
C: Restrictions in an access point policy apply only to requests made through that access point; attaching an access point does not change how the bucket handles requests made directly to it.
Q: Does an access point policy restrict requests sent directly to the bucket?
V: v1=only to requests made through that access point
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-points-policies] Restrictions that you include in an access point policy apply only to requests made through that access point. Attaching an access point to a bucket does not change underlying resource's behavior.
=== s3-access-point-vpc-endpoint-policy-both | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S6
C: To use a VPC-restricted access point, the VPC endpoint policy must grant access to both the access point and the underlying bucket; requests through the access point succeed only then.
Q: What must the VPC endpoint policy allow for a VPC access point to work?
V: both=only succeed through an access point if the VPC endpoint policy grants access to both the access point and the underlying bucket
S: scope='per VPC endpoint' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-points-vpc] To use an access point with a VPC, you must modify the access policy for your VPC endpoint. VPC endpoints allow traffic to flow from your VPC to Amazon S3. They have access control policies that control how resources within the VPC are allowed to interact with Amazon S3. Requests from your VPC to Amazon S3 only succeed through an access point if the VPC endpoint policy grants access to both the access point and the underlying bucket.
=== s3-access-point-vpc-only | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1,1.3.S6
C: Each access point has its own policy and its own Block Public Access settings, and any access point can be configured to accept requests only from one VPC, which restricts data access to a private network.
Q: Can an S3 access point be limited to one VPC?
V: vpc-only=accept requests only from a virtual private cloud (VPC) || own-policy=each access point has its own access point policy
S: scope='per access point' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Welcome] Each access point has its own access point policy. You can configure Block Public Access settings for each access point attached to a bucket. To restrict Amazon S3 data access to a private network, you can also configure any access point to accept requests only from a virtual private cloud (VPC).
=== s3-access-point-vs-bucket-policy-vpc-lockdown | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1,1.3.S6
C: Bucket policies can still limit a bucket to specified VPCs, but access points give an easier, auditable way to lock all or part of a shared dataset to VPC-only traffic; an Organizations SCP can require that every new access point sets its network origin to vpc.
Q: Why use access point network origin controls instead of a VPC condition in the bucket policy?
V: why=an easier, auditable way to lock down all or a subset of data in a shared data set to VPC-only traffic || scp=mandate that any access point created in your organization set the “network origin control” API parameter value to “vpc”
S: scope='access control design choice' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] You can continue to use bucket policies to limit bucket access to specified VPCs. Access points provide an easier, auditable way to lock down all or a subset of data in a shared data set to VPC-only traffic for all applications in your organization using API controls. You can use an AWS Organizations Service Control Policy (SCP) to mandate that any access point created in your organization set the “network origin control” API parameter value to “vpc”.
=== s3-access-points-object-operations-only | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1,1.3.S6
C: Access points can be used only for operations on objects, such as GetObject and PutObject, not for other bucket operations like deleting buckets or creating replication configurations. When attached to a bucket, the access point policy works together with the bucket policy.
Q: What can and cannot be done through an S3 access point?
V: objects-only=you can only use access points to perform operations on objects || with-bucket=the access point policy works in conjunction with the underlying bucket policy
S: scope='per access point' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-points] You can only use access points to perform operations on objects. You can't use access points to perform other Amazon S3 operations, such as deleting buckets or creating S3 Replication configurations.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-points] If your access point is attached to a bucket the access point policy works in conjunction with the underlying bucket policy. You can configure any access point to accept requests only from a virtual private cloud (VPC) to restrict Amazon S3 data access to a private network. You can also configure custom block public access settings for each access point.

=== s3-access-points-scale-policy-space | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1,1.3.S6
C: IAM and bucket policies are the simplest approach for a small to medium number of datasets but are bounded by the 20 KB bucket policy and 5 KB IAM policy size limits; access points give far more policy space because each access point has its own policy of up to 20 KB and there can be thousands per Region per account.
Q: When does one large bucket policy stop scaling, and how do access points help?
V: limit=policy size limits of S3 bucket policies (20 KB) and IAM policies (5 KB) || more=significantly more space for policy statements || many=thousands of access points per AWS Region per account, with a policy up to 20 KB in size for each access point
S: scope='per account' dom='max' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-grants] This strategy works, so long as the necessary policies fit within the policy size limits of S3 bucket policies (20 KB) and IAM policies (5 KB), and within the number of IAM principals allowed per account.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-grants] An approach that offers significantly more space for policy statements is to use S3 Access Points as additional endpoints for S3 buckets, because each access point can have its own policy. You can define quite granular access control patterns, because you can have thousands of access points per AWS Region per account, with a policy up to 20 KB in size for each access point.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/acl-overview] Amazon S3 access control lists (ACLs) enable you to manage access to buckets and objects. Each bucket and object has an ACL attached to it as a subresource. It defines which AWS accounts or groups are granted access and the type of access.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/acl-overview] When you create a bucket or an object, Amazon S3 creates a default ACL that grants the resource owner full control over the resource.

With the Bucket owner enforced setting enabled, requests to set access control lists (ACLs) or update ACLs fail and return the `AccessControlListNotSupported` error code. === s3-archive-classes-access-model | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K10,4.1.S8,3.1.S1
C: S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval gives millisecond retrieval for rarely accessed long-term data and is available for real-time access. S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval and S3 Glacier Deep Archive are archived and not available for real-time access; the archive classes require minimum storage durations and retrieval fees.
Q: Which Glacier classes need a restore before access, and which gives millisecond access?
V: instant=requires milliseconds retrieval || archived=archived, and not available for real-time access || fees=require minimum storage durations and retrieval fees
S: scope='S3 Glacier storage classes' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/storage-class-intro] These storage classes require minimum storage durations and retrieval fees making them most effective for rarely accessed data.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/storage-class-intro] **S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval** (`GLACIER_IR`) – Use for long-term data that's rarely accessed and requires milliseconds retrieval. Data in this storage class is available for real-time access.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/storage-class-intro] However, S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval and S3 Glacier Deep Archive objects are archived, and not available for real-time access.

=== s3-archive-metadata-overhead | pricing-dimension | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-numbers | bul=4.1.S8,4.1.S10
C: Each object archived to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive carries 40 KB of extra metadata: 8 KB billed at S3 Standard rates (name and metadata, so the object still lists in real time) and 32 KB billed at the Glacier class rate (index data needed to restore it).
Q: How much per-object overhead is billed for objects in S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval and Deep Archive, and at what rates?
V: total=40 KB || standard=8 KB || glacier=32 KB
S: scope='per object archived to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive' dom='fixed' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] The minimum storage duration charges (90 days for S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval, 180 days for S3 Glacier Deep Archive) and the per-object storage overhead (40 KB total, of which 8 KB is charged at S3 Standard rates and 32 KB at the destination Glacier rate) also begin at this point.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] For each object archived to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive, Amazon S3 uses 8 KB of storage for the name of the object and other metadata. Amazon S3 stores this metadata so that you can get a real-time list of your archived objects by using the Amazon S3 API.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] For each object that is archived to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive, Amazon S3 adds 32 KB of storage for index and related metadata. This extra data is necessary to identify and restore your object. You are charged S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive rates for this additional storage.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] The minimal storage duration period is 90 days for the S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval storage class and 180 days for S3 Glacier Deep Archive.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] If you delete or overwrite an archived object within the minimal duration period, Amazon S3 charges a prorated early deletion fee.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/restoring-objects-retrieval-options] Provisioned capacity units are allocated to an AWS account. Thus, the requester of the Expedited data retrieval should purchase the provisioned capacity unit, not the bucket owner.
=== s3-archive-provisioned-capacity-unit | capability | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-numbers | bul=4.1.S6
C: One unit of provisioned capacity guarantees at least 3 Expedited retrievals every 5 minutes from S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval and up to 300 megabytes per second of retrieval throughput; without it, Expedited retrievals might not be accepted during periods of high demand.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/restoring-objects-retrieval-options] Without provisioned capacity, Expedited retrievals might not be accepted during periods of high demand. For predictable, fast access to more of your data, consider using the [S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval](https://aws.amazon.com/s3/storage-classes/glacier/instant-retrieval/) storage class.
=== s3-archive-restore-needed-flexible-deep | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.S8,4.1.K10

=== s3-archive-restore-request-rate | quota | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-numbers | bul=4.1.S6
C: Restore requests from the S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval, S3 Glacier Deep Archive and S3 Intelligent-Tiering storage classes are limited to 1,000 transactions per second per account; above that rate requests are throttled with a ThrottlingException, and S3 Batch Operations can restore many objects in one request.
Q: How many restore requests per second does an account get for archived objects?
V: rate=1,000 transactions per second
S: scope='per AWS account' dom='max' adj=False cond='RestoreObject requests for S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval, S3 Glacier Deep Archive or S3 Intelligent-Tiering objects'
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/restoring-objects-retrieval-options] S3 Glacier supports restore requests at a rate of 1,000 transactions per second. If this rate is exceeded otherwise valid requests are throttled or rejected and Amazon S3 returns a `ThrottlingException` error. You can use S3 Batch Operations to retrieve many objects with a single request, which fully utilizes the restore request rate available in your account.

=== s3-archive-restore-temporary-copy | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.S8,4.1.K10
C: A restore makes a temporary copy of an archived object that is available only for the number of days you give in the restore request; afterwards S3 deletes the copy and the object stays archived, and its storage class still reads as Glacier Flexible Retrieval or Deep Archive. While restored you pay for both the archive and the temporary copy at the S3 Standard rate, and S3 processes one restore request at a time per object.
Q: What does a restore from Glacier Flexible Retrieval or Deep Archive produce, and for how long?
V: copy=you must first restore a temporary copy of it || duration=available only for the duration that you specify in the restore request || class=the object's storage class remains S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive || one-at-a-time=processes only one restore request at a time per object
S: scope='per archived object' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] Archived objects are Amazon S3 objects, but before you can access an archived object, you must first restore a temporary copy of it. The restored object copy is available only for the duration that you specify in the restore request. After that, Amazon S3 deletes the temporary copy, and the object remains archived in S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] After you receive a temporary copy of the restored object, the object's storage class remains S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] When you restore an archive, you are paying for both the archive (S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive rate) and a copy that you restored temporarily (S3 Standard storage rate).
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] You can restore an object copy programmatically or by using the Amazon S3 console. Amazon S3 processes only one restore request at a time per object.
=== s3-archive-restore-throughput | limit | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-21-numbers | bul=4.1.S6
C: S3 Glacier restores large datasets at up to 1–2 petabytes per day per customer account; any object larger than 5 terabytes restores at up to 300 megabytes per second, so a 50-terabyte S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval object could take up to 48 hours.
Q: How fast can an account restore a very large archive, and why does a huge single object take longer?
V: throughput=1–2 petabytes per day || object=300 megabytes per second
S: scope='per customer account' dom='max' adj=True cond='contact AWS Support for higher restoration limits'
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/restoring-objects-retrieval-options] After you initiate restore requests, S3 Glacier supports restoring large datasets at a throughput of up to 1–2 petabytes per day per customer account. For any retrieval option, objects larger than 5 terabytes will take longer to be restored with up to 300 megabytes per second of retrieval throughput. For example, a 50-terabyte S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval object could take up to 48 hours to complete. If you require increased restoration limits, you can contact AWS Support.
=== s3-archive-retrieval-options-three | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.S8
C: A restore chooses one of three retrieval options by speed: Expedited, Standard and Bulk. Standard is the default when no option is given; Bulk is the lowest-cost option; Expedited is a premium option charged at the Expedited rate and is not available for S3 Glacier Deep Archive.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/restoring-objects-retrieval-options] Standard is the default option for requests that do not specify the retrieval option.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/restoring-objects-retrieval-options] **Bulk retrieval** – Access your data by using the lowest-cost retrieval option in S3 Glacier storage classes.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/restoring-objects-retrieval-options] Expedited retrievals are a premium feature and are charged at the Expedited request and retrieval rate.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/restoring-objects-retrieval-options] H{| Storage class or tier | Expedited | Standard (with Batch Operations) | Standard (without Batch Operations) | Bulk |} | S3 Glacier Deep Archive or S3 Intelligent-Tiering Deep Archive Access | Not available | 9-12 hours | Within 12 hours | Within 48 hours |

=== s3-authorization-both-accounts-must-allow | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K1,4.1.K1
C: To act on an S3 resource, an IAM principal needs permission from both its own parent AWS account and the AWS account that owns the resource; for a bucket operation, the bucket owner must have granted the permission.
Q: For cross-account S3 access, must both the identity policy and the bucket policy allow the action?
V: both=permission from both the parent AWS account to which it belongs and the AWS account that owns the resource || bucket-op=the bucket owner has granted permission for the requester to perform the operation
S: scope='per request' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/how-s3-evaluates-access-control] In addition, if the request is for a bucket operation, such as a request to list the bucket content, Amazon S3 must verify that the bucket owner has granted permission for the requester to perform the operation. To perform a specific operation on a resource, an IAM principal needs permission from both the parent AWS account to which it belongs and the AWS account that owns the resource.
=== s3-authorization-evaluation-order | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K1
C: S3 converts the user policy, bucket policy and ACLs into one set of policies and evaluates them in order: user context (the requester's parent account), then bucket context (the bucket owner's policies), then object context (the object owner's policies). An explicit deny by the bucket owner stops the request.
Q: In what order does S3 evaluate policies when it authorizes a request?
V: all=evaluates all the relevant access policies, user policies, and resource-based policies (bucket policy, bucket access control list (ACL), and object ACL) || deny=if there is an explicit deny set, Amazon S3 does not authorize the request || contexts=user context
S: scope='per request' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/how-s3-evaluates-access-control] Amazon S3 evaluates all the relevant access policies, user policies, and resource-based policies (bucket policy, bucket access control list (ACL), and object ACL) in deciding whether to authorize the request.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/how-s3-evaluates-access-control] If the request is for a bucket operation, the requester must have permission from the bucket owner. If the request is for an object, Amazon S3 evaluates all the policies owned by the bucket owner to check if the bucket owner has not explicitly denied access to the object. If there is an explicit deny set, Amazon S3 does not authorize the request.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/how-s3-evaluates-access-control] **User context** – In the user context, the parent account to which the user belongs is the context authority.

=== s3-batch-operations-definition | capability | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.S1
C: S3 Batch Operations runs one operation on a list of objects you specify; a single job can act on billions of objects, and S3 tracks progress, sends notifications and stores a completion report.
Q: What is S3 Batch Operations?
V: what=perform a single operation on lists of Amazon S3 objects that you specify || scale=a single job can perform a specified operation on billions of objects || managed=tracks progress, sends notifications, and stores a detailed completion report
S: scope='per Batch Operations job' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/batch-ops] S3 Batch Operations can perform a single operation on lists of Amazon S3 objects that you specify. A single job can perform a specified operation on billions of objects containing exabytes of data. Amazon S3 tracks progress, sends notifications, and stores a detailed completion report of all actions, providing a fully managed, auditable, and serverless experience.
=== s3-batch-operations-manifest | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.S1

S: scope='per Batch Operations job' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/batch-ops] A manifest is an Amazon S3 object that contains the object keys that you want Amazon S3 to act upon. If you want to create a Batch Operations job, you must supply a manifest. Your user-generated manifest must contain the bucket name, object key, and optionally, the object version for each object. If you supply a user-generated manifest, it must be in the form of an Amazon S3 Inventory report or a CSV file.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/batch-ops] You can also have Amazon S3 generate a manifest automatically based on object filter criteria that you specify when you create your job.

=== s3-best-practice-wildcard-principal-means-anyone | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=1.3.K1
C: A bucket policy that allows a wildcard identity, "Principal": "*", effectively means anyone; a wildcard action "*" allows any action in the bucket. Both are signs of a public or over-permissive bucket.
Q: What does Principal "*" mean in a bucket policy?

S: scope='bucket policies' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/security-best-practices] Identify Amazon S3 bucket policies that allow a wildcard identity such as `"Principal": "*"` (which effectively means "anyone"). Also look for policies that allow a wildcard action `"*"` (which effectively allows the user to perform any action in the Amazon S3 bucket).

=== s3-block-public-access-four-settings-most-restrictive | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K1
C: Block Public Access has four independent settings (BlockPublicAcls, IgnorePublicAcls, BlockPublicPolicy, RestrictPublicBuckets) that can be applied to an access point, a bucket or a whole account in any combination. When the settings at different levels differ, S3 applies the most restrictive combination.
Q: What are the four Block Public Access settings, and which level wins when bucket and account settings differ?
V: four=four settings || restrictive=applies the most restrictive combination of the access point, bucket, and account settings || names=BlockPublicAcls || names-2=IgnorePublicAcls || names-3=BlockPublicPolicy || names-4=RestrictPublicBuckets
S: scope='per access point, bucket or account' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-control-block-public-access] Amazon S3 Block Public Access provides four settings. These settings are independent and can be used in any combination. Each setting can be applied to an access point, a bucket, or an entire AWS account.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-control-block-public-access] If the block public access settings for the access point, bucket, or account differ, then Amazon S3 applies the most restrictive combination of the access point, bucket, and account settings.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-control-block-public-access] H{| Name | Description |} | IgnorePublicAcls | Setting this option to `TRUE` causes Amazon S3 to ignore all public ACLs on a bucket and any objects that it contains. This setting enables you to safely block public access granted by ACLs while still allowing `PutObject` calls that include a public ACL (as opposed to `BlockPublicAcls`, which rejects `PutObject` calls that include a public ACL). Enabling this setting doesn't affect the persistence of any existing ACLs and doesn't prevent new public ACLs from being set. Access points don't have ACLs associated with them. If you apply this setting to an access point, it acts as a passthrough to the underlying bucket. If an access point has this setting enabled, requests made through the access point behave as though the underlying bucket has this setting enabled, regardless of whether the bucket actually has this setting enabled.  |
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-control-block-public-access] H{| Name | Description |} | RestrictPublicBuckets | Setting this option to `TRUE` restricts access to an access point or bucket with a public policy to only AWS service principals and authorized users within the bucket owner's account and access point owner's account. This setting blocks all cross-account access to the access point or bucket (except by AWS service principals), while still allowing users within the account to manage the access point or bucket.<br />Enabling this setting doesn't affect existing access point or bucket policies, except that Amazon S3 blocks public and cross-account access derived from any public access point or bucket policy, including non-public delegation to specific accounts. |

=== s3-change-conditional-deletes-2025-09 | change | hard=True | reference | frequently-changing | by=mine-21-changes | bul=4.1.S1
C: On 2025-09-16 S3 added conditional deletes in general purpose buckets: a delete sent with the if-match header and an ETag succeeds only if the ETag matches the object, and the s3:if-match condition key in a bucket policy can require the header on DeleteObject and DeleteObjects requests.
Q: How can S3 prevent a delete of an object that changed since you read it, and since when?
V: date=2025-09-16 || what=conditional deletes
S: scope='general purpose buckets' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/WhatsNew] H{| Change | Description | Date |} | [Amazon S3 now supports conditional deletes in general purpose buckets](#WhatsNew) | Amazon S3 now supports conditional deletes in general purpose buckets. Conditional deletes verify that an object is unchanged before deleting it. Using conditional deletes helps you to prevent accidental deletions in high-concurrency, multiple-writer scenarios. You can now perform conditional deletes using the HTTP `if-match` header with an ETag value. Amazon S3 only allows your delete request to succeed if the Etag provided matches that of the object. Additionally, you can use the `s3:if-match` condition key in your S3 bucket policies to enforce conditional delete operations. For example, you can require clients to use the HTTP `if-match` header in both `DeleteObject` and `DeleteObjects` API operation requests, helping you to minimize the risk of accidentally deleting objects in your bucket. For more information, see [How to perform conditional deletes](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/conditional-deletes.html). | September 16, 2025 |
CI: ["docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/WhatsNew#22", "whats-new-v2#launch-wtqeoqcltrdyavgacstbjb"]

With this header, Amazon S3 compares the provided ETag value with the ETag value of the object in S3. If the ETag values don't match, the operation fails. For more information, see [How to prevent object overwrites with conditional writes](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/conditional-writes.html). | November 25, 2024 |

=== s3-change-crc64nvme-default-checksum-2024-12 | change | hard=True | reference | frequently-changing | by=mine-21-changes | bul=4.1.S1
C: On 2024-12-01 S3 added the CRC-64NVME checksum algorithm and improved checksum integrity features. CRC64NVME is now the default checksum algorithm; for uploads, all AWS-owned clients calculate a checksum and send it with the request, and S3 calculates its own checksum and validates it before storing the object.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/checking-object-integrity] The `CRC64NVME` checksum algorithm is the default checksum algorithm used for checksum calculations.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/checking-object-integrity] For uploads, all AWS-owned clients calculate a checksum of the object and send it with the upload request.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/checking-object-integrity] S3 then independently calculates a checksum value of the object on the server-side, and validates it with the provided value before storing the object and checksum value.

=== s3-change-max-object-size-50-tb-2025-12 | change | hard=True | core | changed-since-2022 | by=mine-21-changes | bul=3.1.K2
C: On 2025-12-02 Amazon S3 raised the maximum size of an object from 5 TB to 50 TB. Objects that large must be uploaded with the Multipart Upload API, and because a single GET request is limited to 5 TB, they are downloaded with concurrent GetObject requests. The largest object that a single PUT can upload is still 5 GB.

For uploading these larger objects, you must implement the Multipart Upload REST API to upload objects in parts, and when downloading these objects, use concurrent `GetObject` requests, as single GET requests are limited to 5 TB. For more information, see [Uploading objects](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/upload-objects.html) and [Downloading objects](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/download-objects.html). | December 2, 2025 |

=== s3-change-sse-c-disabled-default-2026-04 | change | hard=True | core | frequently-changing | by=mine-21-changes | bul=1.3.S2
C: In April 2026 Amazon S3 deployed a default that disables SSE-C for new write requests on all new general purpose buckets, and on existing buckets in accounts that had no SSE-C encrypted objects. Applications that need SSE-C must enable it with the PutBucketEncryption API after creating the bucket. New buckets in Middle East (Bahrain) and Middle East (UAE) are excepted.
Q: Is SSE-C allowed by default on a new S3 bucket today, since when, and how do you allow it?
V: date=April 2026 || rule=SSE-C encryption disabled for all new write requests
S: scope='general purpose buckets' dom='default' adj=True cond='existing buckets in accounts with SSE-C objects were not changed; Middle East (Bahrain) and Middle East (UAE) excepted'
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/default-s3-c-encryption-setting-faq] In April 2026, Amazon S3 deployed an update so all new general purpose buckets have SSE-C encryption disabled for all new write requests.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/default-s3-c-encryption-setting-faq] For existing buckets in AWS accounts with no SSE-C encrypted objects, Amazon S3 also disabled SSE-C for all new write requests.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/default-s3-c-encryption-setting-faq] With this change, applications that need SSE-C encryption must deliberately enable SSE-C by using the [PutBucketEncryption](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/AmazonS3/latest/API/API_PutBucketEncryption.html) API operation after creating a new bucket.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/default-s3-c-encryption-setting-faq] All newly created buckets in all AWS Regions except Middle East (Bahrain) and Middle East (UAE) will have SSE-C disabled by default.

=== s3-change-update-object-encryption-2026-01 | change | hard=True | extended | frequently-changing | by=mine-21-changes | bul=1.3.S2
C: On 2026-01-29 Amazon S3 added the UpdateObjectEncryption API, which changes the server-side encryption type of encrypted objects without any data movement, for example from SSE-S3 to SSE-KMS; with S3 Batch Operations it can standardize the encryption type of entire buckets while keeping object properties and Lifecycle eligibility.
Q: Can the encryption type of existing S3 objects be changed in place, and since when?
V: date=2026-01-29 || what=without any data movement
S: scope='encrypted objects in general purpose buckets' dom='' adj=False cond='objects must already be encrypted'
E: [www/about-aws/whats-new/2026/01/change-the-server-side-encryption-type-of-s3-objects] You can now change the server-side encryption type of encrypted objects in Amazon S3 without any data movement.
E: [www/about-aws/whats-new/2026/01/change-the-server-side-encryption-type-of-s3-objects] With UpdateObjectEncryption, customers can now change the encryption type of existing encrypted objects to move from Amazon S3 managed server-side encryption (SSE-S3) to use server-side encryption with AWS KMS keys (SSE-KMS).
E: [www/about-aws/whats-new/2026/01/change-the-server-side-encryption-type-of-s3-objects] With S3 Batch Operations, you can use UpdateObjectEncryption at scale to standardize the encryption type on entire buckets of objects while preserving object properties and S3 Lifecycle eligibility.
E: [www/about-aws/whats-new/2026/01/change-the-server-side-encryption-type-of-s3-objects] HD{Change the server-side encryption type of Amazon S3 objects} Posted on: Jan 29, 2026

=== s3-dsse-kms-two-layers | capability | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S2
C: DSSE-KMS applies two layers of encryption to an object without the infrastructure that client-side encryption needs; each layer uses a different implementation of 256-bit AES-GCM, and KMS generates and manages the keys.
Q: What is DSSE-KMS for?
V: two=two layers of encryption to your data, without having to invest in infrastructure required for client-side encryption || algo=256-bit Advanced Encryption Standard with Galois Counter Mode (AES-GCM)
S: scope='per object encrypted with DSSE-KMS' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] DSSE-KMS simplifies the process of applying two layers of encryption to your data, without having to invest in infrastructure required for client-side encryption. Each layer of encryption uses a different implementation of the 256-bit Advanced Encryption Standard with Galois Counter Mode (AES-GCM) algorithm and is vetted and accepted for use on top-secret workloads. DSSE-KMS uses AWS KMS to generate data keys, and lets AWS KMS manage your encryption keys.
CI: ["docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/WhatsNew#75"]

=== s3-event-notification-recursive-loop | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.S1
C: A notification whose handler writes to the same bucket that triggers it can cause an execution loop; AWS advises using two buckets or limiting the trigger to a prefix for incoming objects.
Q: How do you avoid a Lambda function that writes to its own trigger bucket invoking itself in a loop?
V: v1=could cause an execution loop || v2=use two buckets
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/EventNotifications] If your notification writes to the same bucket that triggers the notification, it could cause an execution loop. For example, if the bucket triggers a Lambda function each time an object is uploaded, and the function uploads an object to the bucket, then the function indirectly triggers itself. To avoid this, use two buckets, or configure the trigger to only apply to a prefix used for incoming objects.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Welcome] Amazon S3 provides strong read-after-write consistency for PUT and DELETE requests of objects in your Amazon S3 bucket in all AWS Regions. This behavior applies to both writes of new objects as well as PUT requests that overwrite existing objects and DELETE requests.

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Normal Amazon S3 pricing applies when your storage is accessed by another AWS Account. Alternatively, you may choose to configure your bucket as a Requester Pays bucket, in which case the requester will pay the cost of requests and downloads of your Amazon S3 data.

=== s3-faq-crr-vs-mrap | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.S5
C: CRR moves data across Regions to create isolated replicas, while Multi-Region Access Points route requests across Regions; together they give a replicated multi-Region dataset behind a single global endpoint.
Q: What is the difference between CRR and Multi-Region Access Points?
V: v=single global endpoint
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 Multi-Region Access Points help you to manage requests across AWS Regions, while CRR allows you to move data across AWS Regions to create isolated replicas. You use S3 Multi-Region Access Points and CRR together to create a replicated multi-Region dataset that is addressable by a single global endpoint.

=== s3-faq-deep-archive-price | price | hard=True | core | frequently-changing | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S10,4.1.S8

=== s3-faq-deep-archive-retrieval-times | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S8
C: Deep Archive Standard retrieval (the default) returns data within 12 hours, typically starting within 9 hours with S3 Batch Operations; Bulk retrieval typically completes within 48 hours.

V: s=12 hours || b=48 hours || bo=9 hours
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Standard is the default tier and lets you access any of your archived objects within 12 hours, with retrievals typically starting within 9 hours when initiated using S3 Batch Operations. Bulk lets you retrieve large amounts of data, even petabytes of data, inexpensively and typically completes within 48 hours.
=== s3-faq-deep-archive-tape-migration | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S6
C: To move tape archives into S3 Glacier Deep Archive, AWS suggests Tape Gateway (a virtual tape library for existing backup applications), AWS Snowball for terabytes to petabytes, or AWS Direct Connect.
Q: How does AWS recommend migrating tape archives to S3 Glacier Deep Archive?
V: t=Tape Gateway || s=AWS Snowball || d=AWS Direct Connect
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] You can use the AWS Tape Gateway to integrate with existing backup applications using a virtual tape library (VTL) interface. This interface presents virtual tapes to the backup application.
E: [www/s3/faqs] You can also use AWS Snowball to migrate data. Snowball accelerates moving terabytes to petabytes of data into and out of AWS using physical storage devices designed to be secure for transport.
E: [www/s3/faqs] Finally, you can use AWS Direct Connect to establish dedicated network connections from your premises to AWS.

=== s3-faq-durability-11-nines | sla | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=2.2.K11

E: [www/s3/faqs] Based on its unique architecture, S3 is designed to provide 99.999999999% (11 nines) data durability.
=== s3-faq-durability-integrity-checks | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=2.2.K11
C: S3 checks integrity end to end on every upload, confirms the data is stored redundantly before the upload succeeds, and keeps checking data at rest over time.
Q: How does S3 achieve its durability in practice?
V: v=end-to-end integrity checking
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 has end-to-end integrity checking on every object upload and verifies that all data is correctly and redundantly stored across multiple storage devices before it considers your upload to be successful. Once your data is stored in S3, S3 continuously monitors data durability over time with periodic integrity checks of all data at rest.
=== s3-faq-durability-not-delete-protection | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S6
C: S3 durability does not protect against accidental or malicious deletes; you still need to decide what to keep and which controls protect against wrong deletes.
Q: Does S3 durability mean you do not need backups?
V: v=does not protect against accidental or malicious deletes
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Yes. Amazon S3’s durability system does not protect against accidental or malicious deletes. S3 relies on customers to decide what data they want to keep, what data they want to get rid of, and what optional controls they need to protect against deletes that are incorrect, either due to accidents or malice.

=== s3-faq-flexible-retrieval-times | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S8

V: e=1-5 minutes || s=3-5 hours || b=5-12 hours
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] For all but the largest objects (250MB+), data accessed using Expedited retrievals are typically made available within 1-5 minutes. Objects retrieved using Standard retrievals typically complete between 3-5 hours. Standard retrievals typically start in minutes when initiated using S3 Batch Operations. Bulk retrievals typically complete within 5—12 hours, and are free of charge.

V: v=all storage classes except S3 Express One Zone
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] General purpose buckets are the original S3 bucket type, and a single general purpose bucket can contain objects stored across all storage classes except S3 Express One Zone. They are recommended for most use cases and access patterns.
=== s3-faq-glacier-classes-multi-az | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=2.2.K11
C: All S3 Glacier storage classes keep data on multiple devices across physically separated Availability Zones within a Region.
Q: Are the S3 Glacier classes multi-AZ?
V: v=multi-Availability Zone (AZ) resiliency
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] All these storage classes provide multi-Availability Zone (AZ) resiliency by redundantly storing data on multiple devices and physically separated AWS Availability Zones in an AWS Region.
=== s3-faq-ia-to-standard-copy-charges | pricing-dimension | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S10

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''

=== s3-faq-instant-retrieval-availability | sla | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=2.2.K11
C: S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval is designed for 99.9% availability, the same as S3 Standard-IA, with service credits if availability is less than 99% in a billing cycle.
Q: What availability is S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval designed for, and when do credits apply?
V: a=99.9% availability || c=less than 99%
S: scope='S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval is designed for 99.999999999% (11 9s) of durability and 99.9% availability, the same as S3 Standard-IA, and carries a service level agreement providing service credits if availability is less than 99% in any billing cycle.
=== s3-faq-instant-retrieval-min-128kb | limit | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S8,4.1.S10

V: v=128KB
S: scope='per object, S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval' dom='min' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval is designed for larger objects and has a minimum object storage charge of 128KB. Objects smaller than 128KB in size will incur storage charges as if the object were 128KB.
=== s3-faq-instant-retrieval-min-90-days | limit | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S8,4.1.S10
C: S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval objects have a minimum of 90 days of storage; an object deleted, overwritten or transitioned before 90 days incurs a pro-rated charge for the remaining days.
Q: What is the minimum storage duration for S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval?
V: v=90 days
S: scope='per object, S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval' dom='min' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Objects that are archived to S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval have a minimum of 90 days of storage, and objects deleted, overwritten, or transitioned before 90 days incur a pro-rated charge equal to the storage charge for the remaining days.
=== s3-faq-instant-retrieval-no-restore | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S8
C: S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval gives milliseconds access with the same throughput as S3 Standard and S3 Standard-IA; unlike Flexible Retrieval and Deep Archive, no Restore request is needed.
Q: Do you need a Restore request to read an S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval object?
V: v=do not need to issue a Restore request
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Unlike the S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval and S3 Glacier Deep Archive storage classes, which are designed for asynchronous access, you do not need to issue a Restore request before accessing an object stored in S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval.
=== s3-faq-instant-retrieval-once-a-quarter | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.S8,4.1.K9
C: S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval is ideal for data accessed about once a quarter that still needs milliseconds retrieval, with a lower storage price and slightly higher access costs than S3 Standard-IA.
Q: When is S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval the right class?
V: v=once a quarter
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval is ideal if you have data that is rarely accessed (once a quarter) and requires milliseconds retrieval times. It’s the ideal storage class if you want the same low latency and high throughput performance as S3 Standard-IA, but store data that is accessed less frequently than S3 Standard-IA, with a lower storage price and slightly higher data access costs.
=== s3-faq-intelligent-tiering-default-class | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K10,4.1.S8

V: v=should be the default storage class
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Many workloads have changing (user-generated content), unpredictable (analytics, data lakes), or unknown (new applications) access patterns, and that is why S3 Intelligent-Tiering should be the default storage class to automatically save on storage costs.
=== s3-faq-it-archive-restore-times | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K10
C: Objects in the Archive Access tier return to the Frequent Access tier in 3-5 hours, and within 12 hours from the Deep Archive Access tier, after a Restore request.
Q: How long does a restore from the Intelligent-Tiering archive tiers take?
V: a=3-5 hours || d=12 hours
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] To access an object in the Archive or Deep Archive Access tiers, you need to issue a Restore request and the object will begin moving back to the Frequent Access tier, all within the S3 Intelligent-Tiering storage class. Objects in the Archive Access Tier are moved to the Frequent Access tier in 3-5 hours, objects in the Deep Archive Access tier are moved to the Frequent Access tier within 12 hours.
=== s3-faq-it-archive-tiers-90-180 | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K10
C: With the optional archive tiers enabled, objects not accessed for a minimum of 90 days move to the Archive Access tier (up to 71% saving) and after 180 days to the Deep Archive Access tier (up to 95%).
Q: When do Intelligent-Tiering objects move into the optional Archive Access and Deep Archive Access tiers?
V: a=minimum of 90 days || d=180 days || s1=71% || s2=95%
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Once you enable one or both of the asynchronous archive access tiers, S3 Intelligent-Tiering will move objects that have not been accessed for a minimum of 90 days to the Archive Access tier to save up to 71% and after 180 days of no access to the Deep Archive Access tier to save up to 95% for rarely accessed objects.

=== s3-faq-it-tiers-30-90-days | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K10

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] For a low monthly object monitoring and automation charge, S3 Intelligent-Tiering monitors access patterns and automatically moves objects that have not been accessed for 30 consecutive days to the Infrequent Access tier to save up to 40% on storage costs. After 90 days consecutive days of no access, objects are moved to the Archive Instant Access tier to save up to 68% on storage costs.

V: v=incomplete multipart uploads
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] For example, if your application uploads several multipart object parts, but never commits them, you will still be charged for that storage. This policy can lower your S3 storage bill by automatically removing incomplete multipart uploads and the associated storage after a predefined number of days.

=== s3-faq-lifecycle-what | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K7,4.1.S5

V: v=multipart upload expiration
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] You can also set lifecycle expiration policies to automatically remove objects based on the age of the object. You can set a policy for multipart upload expiration, which expires incomplete multipart uploads based on the age of the upload.
=== s3-faq-macie-sensitive-data | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.K1

=== s3-faq-mrap-17-regions | limit | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.S5
C: A Multi-Region Access Point routes across one bucket per Region in up to 17 Regions, and S3 generates a single DNS-compatible name used as the global endpoint.
Q: How many Regions can one Multi-Region Access Point span?
V: v=up to 17 AWS Regions
S: scope='per Multi-Region Access Point' dom='max' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] You can configure your Multi-Region Access Point to route across one bucket per AWS Region, in up to 17 AWS Regions. When you create a Multi-Region Access Point, S3 automatically generates a DNS-compatible name. This name is used as a global endpoint that can be used by your clients.
=== s3-faq-mrap-failover-controls | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.S5,2.2.K11
C: Multi-Region Access Points run active-active or active-passive; in active-passive, failover controls shift S3 request traffic to the alternate Region and account within minutes.
Q: How do Multi-Region Access Point failover controls work?
V: v=active-passive || w=within minutes
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] To directly control this routing, you can operate S3 Multi-Region Access Points in an active-active or active-passive configuration. In an active-passive configuration, you can use S3 Multi-Region Access Points failover controls to initiate a failover to shift S3 data access request traffic to the chosen alternate AWS Region and account within minutes.
=== s3-faq-mrap-global-accelerator | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.S5,3.1.S1
C: Multi-Region Access Points, based on AWS Global Accelerator, route requests over the AWS network to the lowest-latency replicated copy and improve performance by up to 60%.
Q: What is an S3 Multi-Region Access Point?
V: p=up to 60% || g=Based on AWS Global Accelerator
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Amazon S3 Multi-Region Access Points accelerate performance by up to 60% when accessing data sets that are replicated across multiple AWS Regions. Based on AWS Global Accelerator, S3 Multi-Region Access Points consider factors like network congestion and the location of the requesting application to dynamically route your requests over the AWS network to the lowest latency copy of your data.
=== s3-faq-mrap-requester-pays | capability | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K1

Q: Does Requester Pays work with Multi-Region Access Points?
V: v=Requester Pays buckets
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Yes, you can configure the underlying buckets of the S3 Multi-Region Access Point to be Requester Pays buckets. With Requester Pays, the requester pays all of the cost associated to the endpoint usage, including the cost for requests and data transfer cost associated with both the bucket and the Multi-Region Access Point.

=== s3-faq-object-lock-modes | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.K3

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] When deployed in Governance Mode, AWS accounts with specific IAM permissions are able to remove WORM protection from an object version. If you require stronger immutability in order to comply with regulations, you can use Compliance Mode. In Compliance Mode, WORM protection cannot be removed by any user, including the root account.

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Additionally, to replicate objects from S3 Object Lock enabled buckets, your destination bucket must also have S3 Object Lock enabled.
=== s3-faq-object-lock-replication-permissions | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.K3
C: Replicating from an Object Lock bucket needs the permissions s3:GetObjectRetention and s3:GetObjectLegalHold on the source bucket in the replication role; an s3:Get* permission also satisfies this.
Q: What extra permissions does replication from an Object Lock bucket need?
V: v=s3:GetObjectRetention and s3:GetObjectLegalHold
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Yes, to replicate objects from S3 Object Lock enabled buckets you need to grant two new permissions, s3:GetObjectRetention and s3:GetObjectLegalHold, on the source bucket in the IAM role that you use to set up replication. Alternatively, if the IAM role has an s3:Get* permission, it satisfies the requirement.
=== s3-faq-object-lock-replication-retention | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.K3,1.3.S5
C: S3 Replication copies locked objects together with their retention metadata; replicated objects take the Object Lock configuration of the source bucket.
Q: Is Object Lock retention copied to the replica?
V: v=retention metadata
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock-managing] You can use Object Lock with S3 Replication to enable automatic, asynchronous copying of locked objects and their retention metadata, across S3 buckets. This means that for replicated objects, Amazon S3 takes the object lock configuration of the source bucket.
=== s3-faq-object-lock-retention-types | behavior | hard=True | extended | frequently-changing | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.K3
C: Object Lock has two retention types: fixed, where you set the Retain Until Date, and variable, where you turn on an event hold and S3 sets the Retain Until Date when you release the hold.
Q: What are the fixed and variable retention types in Object Lock?
V: v=Retain Until Date || w=event hold
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] With fixed retention, you specify the Retain Until Date when you apply the retention period. With variable retention, you defer the Retain Until Date — you turn on an event hold, and Amazon S3 sets the Retain Until Date when you release the hold.

Q: What does S3 Object Lock do?

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Amazon S3 Object Lock is an Amazon S3 feature that prevents an object version from being deleted or overwritten for a period of time that you define or indefinitely, so that you can enforce retention policies as an added layer of data protection or for regulatory compliance.

=== s3-faq-replica-modification-sync | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.S5
C: By default replication copies metadata from the source objects to the replicas only; with replica modification sync, metadata changes made on replicas are replicated back to the source, making replication two-way.
Q: What does replica modification sync do?
V: v=replica modification sync || w=two-way replication
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/replication-for-metadata-changes] By default, Amazon S3 replicates metadata from the source objects to the replicas only. When replica modification sync is enabled, Amazon S3 replicates metadata changes made to the replica copies back to the source object, making the replication bidirectional (two-way replication).

For CRR, you also pay for inter-region Data Transfer OUT From S3 to your destination region.
E: [www/s3/faqs] Data transfer charges only apply for S3 Cross Region Replication (S3 CRR) and S3 Replication Time Control (S3 RTC), there are no data transfer charges for S3 Same Region Replication (S3 SRR).

=== s3-faq-srr-reasons | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.S5

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] SRR helps you address data sovereignty and compliance requirements by keeping a copy of your data in a separate AWS account in the same region as the original. You can use SRR to change account ownership for the replicated objects to protect data from accidental deletion. You can also use SRR to easily aggregate logs from different S3 buckets for in-region processing, or to configure live replication between test and development environments.

AWS KMS provides an audit trail so you can see who used your key to access which object and when, as well as view failed attempts to access data from users without permission to decrypt the data.

=== s3-faq-standard-ia-tier-down | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K7,4.1.S5

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Yes. In addition to using Lifecycle policies to migrate objects from S3 Standard to S3 Standard-IA, you can also set up Lifecycle policies to tier objects from S3 Standard-IA to S3 One Zone-IA, S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval, S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval, and the S3 Glacier Deep Archive storage class.

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 Transfer Acceleration leverages Amazon CloudFront’s globally distributed AWS Edge locations. As data arrives at an AWS Edge Location, data is routed to your Amazon S3 bucket over an optimized network path.

=== s3-faq-ta-no-data-at-edge | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=3.1.S1
C: Transfer Acceleration has the same security as regular S3 transfers, uses standard TCP with no firewall changes, and no data is ever saved at AWS edge locations.
Q: Does S3 Transfer Acceleration store data at edge locations?
V: v=No data is ever saved at AWS Edge locations
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 Transfer Acceleration provides the same security as regular transfers to Amazon S3. All Amazon S3 security features, such as access restriction based on a client’s IP address, are supported as well. S3 Transfer Acceleration communicates with clients over standard TCP and does not require firewall changes. No data is ever saved at AWS Edge locations.
=== s3-faq-ta-not-faster-no-charge | pricing-dimension | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=3.1.S1
C: If AWS determines that Transfer Acceleration is not likely to be faster than a regular transfer, it does not charge for acceleration on that transfer and may bypass the acceleration system.
Q: What happens if S3 Transfer Acceleration would not be faster than a regular upload?
V: v=we will not charge
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] If we determine that S3 Transfer Acceleration is not likely to be faster than a regular Amazon S3 transfer of the same object to the same destination AWS Region, we will not charge for the use of S3 Transfer Acceleration for that transfer, and we may bypass the S3 Transfer Acceleration system for that upload.
=== s3-faq-ta-vs-cloudfront | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=3.1.S1
C: Transfer Acceleration is the better choice when higher throughput is desired; for objects smaller than 1 GB or a data set under 1 GB, AWS says to consider CloudFront’s PUT/POST.
Q: When should you choose CloudFront PUT/POST instead of S3 Transfer Acceleration?
V: v=1 GB
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 Transfer Acceleration optimizes the TCP protocol and adds additional intelligence between the client and the S3 bucket, making S3 Transfer Acceleration a better choice if a higher throughput is desired. If you have objects that are smaller than 1 GB or if the data set is less than 1 GB in size, you should consider using Amazon CloudFront's PUT/POST commands for optimal performance.
=== s3-faq-ta-vs-direct-connect | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=3.1.S1

V: v=distributed client locations over the public internet
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] AWS Direct Connect is a good choice for customers who have a private networking requirement or who have access to AWS Direct Connect exchanges. S3 Transfer Acceleration is best for submitting data from distributed client locations over the public internet, or where variable network conditions make throughput poor.
=== s3-faq-ta-vs-mrap | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=3.1.S1

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] S3 Transfer Accelerator can help accelerate long-distance transfers of larger objects to and from a single Amazon S3 bucket. With S3 Multi-Region Access Points, you can perform similar accelerated transfers using the AWS global network, but across many S3 buckets in multiple AWS Regions for internet-based, VPC-based, and on-premises requests to and from S3.

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''

Then, create a second replication rule from the bucket containing the replicas (`{{amzn-s3-demo-destination-bucket}}`) to the source bucket (`{{amzn-s3-demo-source-bucket}}`). The source and destination buckets can be in the same or different AWS Regions.

=== s3-faq-versioning-delete-owner | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.S5
C: After a DELETE, simple (un-versioned) requests no longer retrieve the object, but all versions remain and can be restored; only the bucket owner can permanently delete a version.
Q: After a delete in a versioned bucket, who can permanently remove a version?
V: v=Only the owner of an Amazon S3 bucket can permanently delete a version
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] When a user performs a DELETE operation on an object, subsequent simple (un-versioned) requests will no longer retrieve the object. However, all versions of that object will continue to be preserved in your Amazon S3 bucket and can be retrieved or restored. Only the owner of an Amazon S3 bucket can permanently delete a version.
=== s3-faq-versioning-preserves | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=1.3.S5,4.1.S6
C: Once Versioning is enabled, S3 preserves existing objects on every PUT, POST, COPY or DELETE; by default a GET returns the most recently written version.
Q: What does S3 Versioning do on overwrite and delete, and which version does GET return?
V: v=most recently written version
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Once you enable Versioning for a bucket, Amazon S3 preserves existing objects anytime you perform a PUT, POST, COPY, or DELETE operation on them. By default, GET requests will retrieve the most recently written version.
=== s3-faq-versioning-rollback-window | capability | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K7,4.1.S5

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''

=== s3-faq-vpce-both-same-vpc | capability | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K1

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''

=== s3-faq-vpce-choose | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K1
C: AWS recommends interface endpoints to reach S3 from on premises or from a VPC in another Region, and gateway endpoints for same-Region VPC access because gateway endpoints are not billed.
Q: When should you pick a gateway endpoint and when an interface endpoint for S3?
V: v=gateway VPC endpoints as they are not billed
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] AWS recommends that you use interface VPC endpoints to access S3 from on-premises or from a VPC in another AWS Region. For resources that are accessing S3 from VPC in the same AWS Region as S3, we recommend using gateway VPC endpoints as they are not billed.
=== s3-faq-vpce-two-types | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-faq | bul=4.1.K1
C: There are two types of VPC endpoints for S3: gateway endpoints, a target in your route table, and interface endpoints, which use private IPs and can be reached from your VPC, on premises, or another Region.
Q: What are the two kinds of VPC endpoint for S3 and how do they differ?
V: v=gateway VPC endpoints and interface VPC endpoints
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] There are two types of VPC endpoints for S3: gateway VPC endpoints and interface VPC endpoints. Gateway endpoints are a gateway that you specify in your route table to access S3 from your VPC over the AWS network. Interface endpoints extend the functionality of gateway endpoints by using private IPs to route requests to S3 from within your VPC, on-premises, or from a different AWS Region.

=== s3-files-vs-efs-vs-fsx | behavior | hard=True | extended | frequently-changing | by=mine-21-changes | bul=3.1.K2
C: AWS positions S3 Files for data that is already in S3 and must be used by file-based applications, AI agents or teams with standard file operations; Amazon EFS for a fully managed shared file system as primary storage where data originates in and is mainly accessed through the file system; and Amazon FSx for existing applications that run on network-attached file storage.
Q: When should you choose S3 Files, Amazon EFS or Amazon FSx?
V: s3-files=you already have data in S3 || efs=primary storage || fsx=run on network-attached file storage
S: scope='choosing file access for AWS compute' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Use S3 Files when you already have data in S3 and you need file-based applications, AI agents, or teams to work with it directly using standard file operations.
E: [www/s3/faqs] Use Amazon EFS when you need a fully managed, cloud-native shared file system as your primary storage for workloads like shared home directories, content management, application configuration, and development environments where your data originates in and is primarily accessed through the file system.
E: [www/s3/faqs] Use Amazon FSx if you have existing file-based applications that run on network-attached file storage.

=== s3-global-bucket-name-freed-on-delete | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=3.1.K2
C: A global general purpose bucket name is unavailable to anyone else in the partition while the bucket exists; when the bucket is deleted, the name becomes available again for anyone to re-create.

V: freed=the bucket name becomes available again in the global namespace for anyone to re-create || taken=unavailable for anyone else to create within the partition
S: scope='shared global namespace, per partition' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/gpbucketnamespaces] After creating a general purpose bucket in the shared global namespace, that bucket name is unavailable for anyone else to create within the partition. When you delete a global general purpose bucket, the bucket name becomes available again in the global namespace for anyone to re-create.

=== s3-int-tiering-automatic-tiers | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-numbers | bul=4.1.K10
C: S3 Intelligent-Tiering moves an object to the Infrequent Access tier after 30 consecutive days without access and to the Archive Instant Access tier after 90 days without access, for a small monthly monitoring and automation charge and with no performance impact.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/intelligent-tiering-overview] For a low monthly object monitoring and automation charge, S3 Intelligent-Tiering monitors access patterns and automatically moves objects to the Infrequent Access tier when they haven't been accessed for 30 consecutive days. After 90 days of no access, the objects are moved to the Archive Instant Access tier without performance impact or operational overhead.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/intelligent-tiering-overview] Other actions **don't** constitute access that automatically moves objects from the Infrequent Access tier or the Archive Instant Access tier back to the Frequent Access tier.

=== s3-int-tiering-optional-archive-tiers | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-numbers | bul=4.1.K10
C: The optional Archive Access tier archives objects not accessed for a minimum of 90 consecutive days and the optional Deep Archive Access tier after a minimum of 180 consecutive days; for both, the number of days can be set up to a maximum of 730 days, and objects in them must be restored with RestoreObject before they can be read.

With Deep Archive Access, S3 Intelligent-Tiering moves objects to the Deep Archive Access tier after a minimum of 180 consecutive days of no access. For both tiers, you can configure the number of days of no access based on your needs.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/intelligent-tiering-overview] You can extend the last access time for archiving to a maximum of 730 days.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/intelligent-tiering-overview] If the object that you are retrieving is stored in the Archive Access or Deep Archive Access tiers, you must first restore the object by using the `RestoreObject` operation.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/mpu-abort-incomplete-mpu-lifecycle-config] When a multipart upload isn't completed within the specified time frame, it becomes eligible for an abort operation. Amazon S3 then stops the multipart upload and deletes the parts associated with the multipart upload. This rule applies to both existing multipart uploads and those that you create later.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/mpu-abort-incomplete-mpu-lifecycle-config] Also, this action doesn't apply to objects. No objects are deleted by this lifecycle action. Additionally, you will not incur early delete charges for S3 Lifecycle when you remove any incomplete multipart upload parts.
=== s3-lifecycle-archive-billing-start | behavior | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-21-numbers | bul=4.1.S5
C: Lifecycle transitions to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval and S3 Glacier Deep Archive run asynchronously, but billing at the destination class rate, its minimum duration and its per-object overhead start on the date the rule is satisfied, even before the physical move; only transitions to S3 Intelligent-Tiering bill after the physical transition completes.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] The only exception is transitions to S3 Intelligent-Tiering, where billing changes occur after the physical transition completes.

S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lifecycle-mgmt] If there is any delay between when an object becomes eligible for a lifecycle action and when Amazon S3 transfers or expires your object, billing changes are applied as soon as the object becomes eligible for the lifecycle action.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lifecycle-mgmt] The one exception to this behavior is if you have a lifecycle rule to transition to the S3 Intelligent-Tiering storage class. In that case, billing changes don't occur until the object has transitioned to S3 Intelligent-Tiering.
=== s3-lifecycle-conflict-cost-optimized | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K7
C: When Lifecycle rules overlap, S3 Lifecycle optimizes for cost: of two overlapping expirations the shorter one is honored, and of two overlapping transitions the lower-cost storage class is chosen.
Q: When two Lifecycle rules overlap, which expiration and which transition does S3 apply?
V: v1=the shorter expiration policy is honored || v2=the lower-cost storage class || v3=optimizes for cost
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-conflicts] Generally, Amazon S3 Lifecycle optimizes for cost. For example, if two expiration policies overlap, the shorter expiration policy is honored so that data is not stored for longer than expected. Likewise, if two transition policies overlap, S3 Lifecycle transitions your objects to the lower-cost storage class.
=== s3-lifecycle-conflict-deletion-wins | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K7

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-expire-general-considerations] When an object is eligible for both an S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval and an S3 Standard-IA (or an S3 One Zone-IA) transition, Amazon S3 chooses the S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval transition.
