=== s3-lifecycle-glacier-overhead-40-kb-per-object | pricing-dimension | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=4.1.S10,4.1.S6
C: Each object archived to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or Deep Archive carries 8 KB of metadata billed at S3 Standard rates plus 32 KB of index metadata billed at the Glacier class rate, so archiving many small objects adds overhead; AWS suggests aggregating small objects first.
Q: Why can archiving millions of tiny objects to Glacier cost more than expected?
V: standard-part=8 KB of storage for the name of the object and other metadata || glacier-part=32 KB of storage for index and related metadata || advice=aggregating many small objects into a smaller number of large objects
S: scope='per object archived to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive' dom='fixed' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] For each object archived to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive, Amazon S3 uses 8 KB of storage for the name of the object and other metadata.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] For each object that is archived to S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive, Amazon S3 adds 32 KB of storage for index and related metadata. This extra data is necessary to identify and restore your object. You are charged S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive rates for this additional storage.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] If you are archiving small objects, consider these storage charges. Also consider aggregating many small objects into a smaller number of large objects to reduce overhead costs.

C: Moving objects out of a storage class before its minimum storage duration is charged for the rest of that duration, and a single Lifecycle rule cannot chain a second transition before the first class's minimum duration has passed.

V: charged=you are charged for the remainder of that duration || no-chain=can't create a single Lifecycle rule that transitions objects from one storage class to another before the minimum storage duration period has passed
S: scope='per Lifecycle rule' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] Certain storage classes have a minimum object storage duration. If you transition objects out of these storage classes before the minimum duration, you are charged for the remainder of that duration.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] You can't create a single Lifecycle rule that transitions objects from one storage class to another before the minimum storage duration period has passed.

=== s3-lifecycle-small-objects-not-transitioned-default | default | hard=True | extended | frequently-changing | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K7
C: By default a Lifecycle configuration does not transition objects smaller than 128 KB to any storage class, because each transition is a charged request and the savings on small objects can be smaller than the cost; an object size filter in the rule lets smaller objects transition.
Q: Why are small objects not moved by Lifecycle rules by default, and how do you change that?
V: default=prevents objects smaller than 128 KB from being transitioned to any storage class || why=you are charged a transition request for each object || override=add an object size filter to your Lifecycle transition rules
S: scope='per Lifecycle configuration' dom='default' adj=True cond='configurations created before September 2024 keep the previous behavior until modified'
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] Amazon S3 applies a default behavior to S3 Lifecycle configurations that prevents objects smaller than 128 KB from being transitioned to any storage class. We don't recommend transitioning objects less than 128 KB because you are charged a transition request for each object. This means, for smaller objects, the transition costs can outweigh the storage savings.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] To allow smaller objects to transition, you can add an object size filter to your Lifecycle transition rules that specifies a custom minimum size (`ObjectSizeGreaterThan`) or maximum size (`ObjectSizeLessThan`).

=== s3-object-lock-bucket-default-retention | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3

=== s3-object-lock-compliance-mode | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3
C: In compliance mode, a protected object version cannot be overwritten or deleted by any user, including the root user; its retention mode cannot be changed and its retention period cannot be shortened. The only way to delete such an object before its retention date is to delete the AWS account.
Q: Who can delete or shorten retention on an object locked in compliance mode?
V: nobody=can't be overwritten or deleted by any user, including the root user in your AWS account || no-shorten=its retention period can't be shortened || only-way=the only way to delete an object under the compliance mode before its retention date expires is to delete the associated AWS account
S: scope='per object version in compliance mode' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] In *compliance* mode, a protected object version can't be overwritten or deleted by any user, including the root user in your AWS account. When an object is locked in compliance mode, its retention mode can't be changed, and its retention period can't be shortened.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] The only way to delete an object under the compliance mode before its retention date expires is to delete the associated AWS account.
=== s3-object-lock-delete-behavior | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3
C: On a locked object, a permanent DELETE that names a version ID returns Access Denied (403 Forbidden), while a simple DELETE without a version ID returns 200 OK and adds a delete marker that becomes the current version. Delete markers are not WORM-protected.
Q: What happens when you try to delete an object protected by Object Lock?
V: permanent=Access Denied (403 Forbidden) || simple=inserts a delete marker in the bucket || markers=delete markers are not WORM-protected
S: scope='per protected object version' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] **Permanent `DELETE` request** – If you issued a permanent `DELETE` request (a request that specifies a version ID), Amazon S3 returns an Access Denied (`403 Forbidden`) error when you try to delete the object.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] **Simple `DELETE` request** – If you issued a simple `DELETE` request (a request that doesn't specify a version ID), Amazon S3 returns a `200 OK` response and inserts a delete marker in the bucket, and that marker becomes the current version of the object with a new ID.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock-managing] Delete markers are not WORM-protected, regardless of any retention period or legal hold in place on the underlying object.

=== s3-object-lock-governance-mode | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3
C: In governance mode, users cannot overwrite or delete a locked object version or change its lock settings unless they have special permission. To override governance retention a user needs s3:BypassGovernanceRetention and must send the x-amz-bypass-governance-retention:true header.
Q: Who can delete an object locked in governance mode, and what do they need?

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] In *governance* mode, users can't overwrite or delete an object version or alter its lock settings unless they have special permissions. With governance mode, you protect objects against being deleted by most users, but you can still grant some users permission to alter the retention settings or delete the objects if necessary.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] To override or remove governance-mode retention settings, you must have the `s3:BypassGovernanceRetention` permission and must explicitly include `x-amz-bypass-governance-retention:true` as a request header with any request that requires overriding governance mode.
=== s3-object-lock-legal-hold-independent | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3
C: A legal hold is independent of the retention period: if the retention period expires, the legal hold keeps protecting the object until an authorized user removes it, and if the hold is removed, the retention period still protects the version until it expires. Any user with the s3:PutObjectLegalHold permission can place or remove a legal hold.
Q: What happens to a locked object when its retention period expires but a legal hold is still on?
V: keeps=the legal hold continues to protect the object until an authorized user explicitly removes the legal hold || permission=s3:PutObjectLegalHold
S: scope='per object version' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] If the retention period expires, the object doesn't lose its WORM protection. Rather, the legal hold continues to protect the object until an authorized user explicitly removes the legal hold. Similarly, if you remove a legal hold while an object version has a retention period in effect, the object version remains protected until the retention period expires.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] Legal holds can be freely placed and removed by any user who has the `s3:PutObjectLegalHold` permission.
=== s3-object-lock-lifecycle-cannot-expire-locked | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3,4.1.K7

However, a locked version of an object cannot be deleted by a S3 Lifecycle expiration policy. Object Lock is maintained regardless of which storage class the object resides in and throughout S3 Lifecycle transitions between storage classes.
=== s3-object-lock-mode-choice | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] Consider using *Compliance mode* if you never want any user, including the root user in your AWS account, to be able to delete the objects during a pre-defined retention period. === s3-object-lock-requires-versioning | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3

Retention periods and legal holds don't prevent new versions of the object from being created, or delete markers to be added on top of the object.

=== s3-object-lock-retention-vs-legal-hold | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3
C: Object Lock has two retention tools: a retention period locks an object version for a fixed period, and a legal hold gives the same protection with no expiration date and stays until you remove it. An object version can have a retention period, a legal hold, or both.
Q: How does a legal hold differ from a retention period, and can both apply at once?
V: both=an object version can have a retention period, a legal hold, or both || legal-hold=a legal hold provides the same protection as a retention period, but it has no expiration date || retention=a fixed period of time during which an object version remains locked
S: scope='per object version' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] Object Lock provides two ways to manage object retention: *retention periods* and *legal holds*. An object version can have a retention period, a legal hold, or both.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] **Retention period** – A retention period specifies a fixed period of time during which an object version remains locked.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/object-lock] **Legal hold** – A legal hold provides the same protection as a retention period, but it has no expiration date. Instead, a legal hold remains in place until you explicitly remove it. Legal holds are independent from retention periods and are placed on individual object versions.
=== s3-object-lock-worm-definition | capability | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3,4.1.S6

=== s3-object-max-size-exact | limit | hard=True | reference | stable | by=mine-21-numbers | bul=3.1.S1

=== s3-object-ownership-default-bucket-owner-enforced | default | hard=True | core | frequently-changing | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K1,4.1.K1
C: S3 Object Ownership is a bucket-level setting. By default it is set to Bucket owner enforced and all ACLs are disabled; the bucket owner then owns every object in the bucket and manages access only with policies.
Q: What is the default Object Ownership setting of a new bucket, and are ACLs on by default?

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Welcome] S3 Object Ownership is an Amazon S3 bucket-level setting that you can use to both control ownership of the objects that are uploaded to your bucket and to disable or enable ACLs. By default, Object Ownership is set to the Bucket owner enforced setting, and all ACLs are disabled. When ACLs are disabled, the bucket owner owns all the objects in the bucket and manages access to them exclusively by using access-management policies.

=== s3-object-ownership-disable-acls-existing-bucket | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K1
C: ACLs can be disabled on new and existing buckets. On an existing bucket with objects, after ACLs are disabled the bucket and object ACLs no longer take part in access evaluation and access is decided by policies; ACLs can be re-enabled later and the earlier ACLs come back.
Q: What happens to existing object ACLs when you disable ACLs on a bucket?
V: no-longer=the object and bucket ACLs are no longer part of an access evaluation || restore=your preexisting bucket and object ACLs are restored
S: scope='per general purpose bucket' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/about-object-ownership] In the case of an existing bucket that already has objects in it, after you disable ACLs, the object and bucket ACLs are no longer part of an access evaluation, and access is granted or denied on the basis of policies. For existing buckets, you can re-enable ACLs at any time after you disable them, and your preexisting bucket and object ACLs are restored.
=== s3-object-ownership-put-with-other-acl-fails | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K1
C: With ACLs disabled, a bucket accepts only PUT requests that specify no ACL or the bucket-owner-full-control ACL; a PUT with any other ACL fails with a 400 error and the code AccessControlListNotSupported.
Q: Which uploads does a bucket with ACLs disabled reject?
V: accepts=accepts only PUT requests that do not specify an ACL or PUT requests with bucket owner full control ACLs || fail=fail and return a 400 error with the error code AccessControlListNotSupported
S: scope='per general purpose bucket' dom='' adj=True cond='Object Ownership set to Bucket owner enforced'
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/about-object-ownership] After you disable ACLs, your bucket accepts only `PUT` requests that do not specify an ACL or `PUT` requests with bucket owner full control ACLs, for example, the `bucket-owner-full-control` canned ACL or equivalent forms of this ACL expressed in XML. Existing applications that support bucket owner full control ACLs see no impact. `PUT` requests that contain other ACLs (for example, custom grants to certain AWS accounts) fail and return a `400` error with the error code `AccessControlListNotSupported`.
=== s3-object-ownership-three-settings | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K1,4.1.K1
C: Object Ownership has three settings: Bucket owner enforced (ACLs disabled, bucket owner owns every object), Bucket owner preferred (bucket owner owns new objects written with the bucket-owner-full-control canned ACL), and Object writer (the uploading account owns the object and can grant access with ACLs).
Q: What are the three Object Ownership settings, and who owns uploaded objects under each?
V: enforced=Bucket owner enforced (default) || preferred=Bucket owner preferred || writer=Object writer || canned=bucket-owner-full-control
S: scope='per general purpose bucket' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/about-object-ownership] **Bucket owner enforced (default)** – ACLs are disabled, and the bucket owner automatically owns and has full control over every object in the bucket. ACLs no longer affect permissions to data in the S3 bucket. The bucket uses policies to define access control.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/about-object-ownership] **Bucket owner preferred** – The bucket owner owns and has full control over new objects that other accounts write to the bucket with the `bucket-owner-full-control` canned ACL.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/about-object-ownership] **Object writer** – The AWS account that uploads an object owns the object, has full control over it, and can grant other users access to it through ACLs.

You can also specify custom metadata at the time that the object is stored.

=== s3-object-tags-uses | capability | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K3,4.1.K7

With these, you have the ability to create AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) policies, set up Amazon S3 Lifecycle policies, and customize storage metrics. These object-level tags can then manage transitions between storage classes and expire objects in the background.
=== s3-object-writer-owns-object-when-acls-enabled | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K1,4.1.K1
C: When ACLs are in use and another account uploads an object to your bucket, that account (the object writer) owns the object and can grant access to it with ACLs. Object Ownership changes this so that ACLs are disabled and the bucket owner automatically owns every object.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/how-s3-evaluates-access-control] By default, when another AWS account uploads an object to your S3 general purpose bucket, that account (the object writer) owns the object, has access to it, and can grant other users access to it through access control lists (ACLs). You can use Object Ownership to change this default behavior so that ACLs are disabled and you, as the bucket owner, automatically own every object in your general purpose bucket.

=== s3-permission-delegation-no-second-cross-account-hop | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=1.3.K1
C: An account that owns an S3 resource can grant permissions to another AWS account, which can then delegate them (or a subset) to its own users; but an account that received permissions from another account cannot delegate them cross-account to a further AWS account.
Q: Can an account that was granted access to a bucket pass that access on to a third account?
V: rule=can't delegate permission cross-account to another AWS account
S: scope='S3 permission delegation' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-policy-language-overview] If an AWS account owns a resource, it can grant those permissions to another AWS account. That account can then delegate those permissions, or a subset of them, to users in the account. This is referred to as* permission delegation*. But an account that receives permissions from another account can't delegate permission cross-account to another AWS account.

A prefix can be any length, subject to the maximum length of the object key name (1,024 bytes). You can think of prefixes as a way to organize your data in a similar way to directories. However, prefixes are not directories.

=== s3-presigned-url-expires-with-temporary-credentials | restriction | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1
C: A presigned URL made with temporary credentials expires when those credentials expire, even if the URL was given a later expiration time. With an assumed IAM role, the URL expires when the role session ends.
Q: Why does a presigned URL made by a Lambda function or EC2 role stop working before its expiration time?
V: rule=the URL expires when the credential expires || role=the presigned URL expires when the role session ends
S: scope='presigned URLs made with temporary security credentials' dom='' adj=False cond='temporary credentials (IAM role, EC2 instance role, STS)'
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/using-presigned-url] If you created a presigned URL using a temporary credential, the URL expires when the credential expires. In general, a presigned URL expires when the credential you used to create it is revoked, deleted, or deactivated. This is true even if the URL was created with a later expiration time.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/using-presigned-url] A presigned URL expires at either its configured expiration time or when its associated credentials expire, whichever occurs first. For Amazon Elastic Container Service tasks or containers, role credentials typically rotate every 1-6 hours. When using AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS) AssumeRole, the presigned URL expires when the role session ends, which by default is 1 hour.
=== s3-presigned-url-network-restriction-source-ip-vpce | capability | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=4.1.K1,1.3.K1
C: To restrict where presigned URLs can be used, write IAM or bucket policies with a network condition: aws:SourceIp for the public S3 endpoint, or aws:SourceVpc / aws:SourceVpce for a VPC endpoint; these restrictions apply to all S3 access, not only presigned URLs.
Q: How do you stop a leaked presigned URL from working outside your network?
V: public=aws:SourceIp || vpc=aws:SourceVpc or aws:SourceVpce || scope=These restrictions also apply outside of the presigned URL scenario
S: scope='IAM principal, bucket, or access point policies' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/using-presigned-url] The IAM global condition key that you use depends on the type of endpoint. If you're using the public endpoint for Amazon S3, use `aws:SourceIp`. If you're using a virtual private cloud (VPC) endpoint to Amazon S3, use `aws:SourceVpc` or `aws:SourceVpce`.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/using-presigned-url] A network-path restriction on the IAM principal requires the user of those credentials to make requests from the specified network. A restriction on the bucket or access point requires that all requests to that resource originate from the specified network. These restrictions also apply outside of the presigned URL scenario.
=== s3-presigned-url-reusable-until-expiry | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K1
C: A presigned URL can be used many times until its expiration date and time. S3 checks the expiration at the time of the HTTP request, so a download that started before expiry continues past it.
Q: Is a presigned URL single-use, and what happens to a download in progress when the URL expires?
V: multi=use the presigned URL multiple times, up to the expiration date and time || check=checks the expiration date and time of a signed URL at the time of the HTTP request
S: scope='per presigned URL' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/using-presigned-url] You can use the presigned URL multiple times, up to the expiration date and time.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/using-presigned-url] Amazon S3 checks the expiration date and time of a signed URL at the time of the HTTP request. For example, if a client begins to download a large file immediately before the expiration time, the download continues even if the expiration time passes during the download.

=== s3-presigned-url-upload-bucket-owner-owns-object | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=4.1.K1
C: When someone uploads through a presigned URL, S3 creates the object in the named bucket, replaces any existing object with the same key, and the bucket owner owns the uploaded object.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/PresignedUrlUploadObject] When someone uses the URL to upload an object, Amazon S3 creates the object in the specified bucket. If an object with the same key that is specified in the presigned URL already exists in the bucket, Amazon S3 replaces the existing object with the uploaded object. After upload, the bucket owner will own the object.

=== s3-pricing-restore-pays-archive-plus-temporary-standard-copy | pricing-dimension | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=4.1.S6

E: [www/s3/pricing] When you restore an archive, you are paying for both the archive (charged at the S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive rate) and a copy, accessible with GET using the same object key, that you restored temporarily (charged at the S3 Standard storage rate for a duration of time you choose).
=== s3-pricing-retrieval-charges-which-classes | pricing-dimension | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=4.1.S10,4.1.S6
C: A retrieval charge applies on GET for S3 Standard-IA, S3 One Zone-IA and S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval; for S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval and S3 Glacier Deep Archive the retrieval is paid as part of the restore request.
Q: Which storage classes charge for retrieval, and when is the charge taken?
V: on-get=S3 Standard – Infrequent Access, S3 One Zone – Infrequent Access, or S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval || on-restore=you pay for retrievals as a part of the restore request
S: scope='per GB retrieved, by storage class' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/pricing] You pay for retrievals when you GET an object stored in the S3 Standard – Infrequent Access, S3 One Zone – Infrequent Access, or S3 Glacier Instant Retrieval storage classes. When you restore an archive from the S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive storage classes, you pay for retrievals as a part of the restore request.

=== s3-public-policy-definition | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.K1
C: S3 treats a bucket or object ACL as public if it grants any permission to the AllUsers or AuthenticatedUsers group. A bucket policy is assumed public unless it grants access only to fixed values such as a principal, a CIDR block with aws:SourceIp, aws:SourceVpc or aws:SourceVpce.
Q: What makes an ACL or bucket policy count as public for Block Public Access?
V: acl=grants any permissions to members of the predefined AllUsers or AuthenticatedUsers groups || policy=begins by assuming that the policy is public
S: scope='Block Public Access evaluation' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-control-block-public-access] Amazon S3 considers a bucket or object ACL public if it grants any permissions to members of the predefined `AllUsers` or `AuthenticatedUsers` groups.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/access-control-block-public-access] When evaluating a bucket policy, Amazon S3 begins by assuming that the policy is public. It then evaluates the policy to determine whether it qualifies as non-public.

=== s3-replication-asynchronous-new-objects | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5,2.2.K11
C: S3 Replication copies objects asynchronously between buckets owned by the same or different accounts. A replication rule replicates new objects written after the rule exists; existing objects, objects that failed earlier and objects that are themselves replicas are copied with S3 Batch Replication.
Q: Does a new replication rule copy the objects that are already in the bucket?
V: async=automatic, asynchronous copying of objects across Amazon S3 buckets || new=replicate new objects written to the bucket || existing=replicate existing bucket contents (S3 Batch Replication)
S: scope='per replication rule' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Amazon S3 Replication enables automatic, asynchronous copying of objects across Amazon S3 buckets. Buckets that are configured for object replication can be owned by the same AWS account or by different accounts. You can replicate new objects written to the bucket to one or more destination buckets between different AWS Regions (S3 Cross-Region Replication), or within the same AWS Region (S3 Same-Region Replication).
E: [www/s3/faqs] You can also replicate existing bucket contents (S3 Batch Replication), including existing objects, objects that previously failed to replicate, and objects replicated from another source.

=== s3-replication-delete-markers-optional | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5
C: Delete markers are replicated only when delete marker replication is enabled in the replication rule; then S3 behaves as if the object was deleted in both buckets. Delete marker replication is not supported for tag-based replication rules.
Q: Are delete markers replicated, and under what condition?
V: if-enabled=if you have delete marker replication enabled in your replication configuration || effect=Amazon S3 will behave as if the object was deleted in both buckets || tags=does not support delete marker replication for object tag based replication rules
S: scope='per replication rule' dom='' adj=True cond='delete marker replication enabled in the rule'
E: [www/s3/faqs] Yes, you can replicate delete markers from source to destination if you have delete marker replication enabled in your replication configuration. When you replicate delete markers, Amazon S3 will behave as if the object was deleted in both buckets. You can enable delete marker replication for a new or existing replication rule.
E: [www/s3/faqs] Amazon S3 Replication does not support delete marker replication for object tag based replication rules.

=== s3-replication-direct-to-colder-class | capability | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=1.3.S5,4.1.S10

You can also replicate your data to the same storage class and use lifecycle configurations on the destination buckets to move your objects to a colder storage class as they age.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/replication-time-control] The AWS KMS request rate quota applies to requests that you make directly and to requests made by Amazon S3 replication on your behalf.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/replication-for-metadata-changes] You must enable replica modification sync on both the source and destination buckets to replicate replica metadata changes like object access control lists (ACLs), object tags, object annotations, or Object Lock settings on the replicated objects.

The default encryption settings of the destination bucket are not used.

=== s3-replication-replicas-not-rereplicated | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5
C: By default S3 does not replicate objects that are themselves replicas created by another replication rule (A to B, then B to C does not copy the replicas from A to C); Batch Replication can replicate them.
Q: If bucket A replicates to B and B replicates to C, do the replicas from A reach C?
V: v1=are not replicated to bucket C || v2=use Batch Replication
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/replication-what-is-isnot-replicated] Objects in the source bucket that are replicas that were created by another replication rule. For example, suppose you configure replication where bucket A is the source and bucket B is the destination. Now suppose that you add another replication configuration where bucket B is the source and bucket C is the destination. In this case, objects in bucket B that are replicas of objects in bucket A are not replicated to bucket C.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/replication-what-is-isnot-replicated] To replicate objects that are replicas, use Batch Replication.

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/replication-time-control] You incur costs for only one `PUT` request per object replicated.

=== s3-replication-two-way-before-mrap-failover | behavior | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=1.3.S5,2.2.K11

Two-way replication rules help ensure that when data is written to the S3 bucket that traffic fails over to, that data is then replicated back to the source bucket.
=== s3-replication-version-deletes-not-replicated | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5

But it doesn't replicate the deletion in the destination buckets. In other words, it doesn't delete the same object version from the destination buckets. This protects data from malicious deletions.

=== s3-restore-temporary-copy-storage-class-unchanged | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=4.1.S6

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/lifecycle-transition-general-considerations] Archived objects aren't accessible in real time. You must first initiate a restore request and then wait until a temporary copy of the object is available for the duration that you specify in the request. After you receive a temporary copy of the restored object, the object's storage class remains S3 Glacier Flexible Retrieval or S3 Glacier Deep Archive.

=== s3-server-side-vs-client-side-encryption | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S2
C: With server-side encryption S3 encrypts objects before writing them to disk and decrypts them on download. With client-side encryption you encrypt data before upload and manage the encryption process, keys and tools yourself.
Q: What is the difference between server-side and client-side encryption in S3?
V: sse=Amazon S3 encrypts your objects before saving them on disks in AWS data centers and then decrypts the objects when you download them || cse=you encrypt your data client-side and upload the encrypted data to Amazon S3 || cse-keys=you manage the encryption process, encryption keys, and related tools
S: scope='per object' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/UsingEncryption] **Server-side encryption** – Amazon S3 encrypts your objects before saving them on disks in AWS data centers and then decrypts the objects when you download them.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/UsingEncryption] **Client-side encryption** – You encrypt your data client-side and upload the encrypted data to Amazon S3. In this case, you manage the encryption process, encryption keys, and related tools.
=== s3-single-key-updates-atomic | behavior | hard=True | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=3.1.K2

S: scope='per object key' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Welcome] Updates to a single key are atomic. For example, if you make a PUT request to an existing key from one thread and perform a GET request on the same key from a second thread concurrently, you will get either the old data or the new data, but never partial or corrupt data.

=== s3-sse-c-impractical-for-sharing | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=1.3.S2
C: SSE-C needs the encryption key in every read or write request, which makes sharing access with other users, roles or AWS services impractical; AWS managed services cannot natively decrypt SSE-C objects. SSE-S3 or SSE-KMS give equal protection with more flexibility.
Q: Why is SSE-C the wrong answer when other AWS services must read the objects?
V: burden=provide the encryption key with every request || limit=cannot be natively decrypted by AWS managed services
S: scope='SSE-C objects' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/security-best-practices] SSE-C requires you to provide the encryption key with every request to read or write encrypted objects. This makes it impractical to share access with other users, roles, or AWS services that operate on your data. Objects encrypted with SSE-C cannot be natively decrypted by AWS managed services. For most workloads, server-side encryption with Amazon S3 managed keys (SSE-S3) or AWS KMS keys (SSE-KMS) provides equivalent protection with greater flexibility.
=== s3-sse-c-key-never-stored-https-required | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-06-numbers+changes | bul=1.3.S2
C: With SSE-C, S3 never stores the encryption key, so you must supply it every time the data is downloaded, and requests with SSE-C headers must use HTTPS.

V: phrase=S3 never stores the encryption key || https=You must use HTTPS
S: scope='SSE-C requests' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/ServerSideEncryptionCustomerKeys] S3 never stores the encryption key when you use SSE-C. You must supply the encryption key every time you want anyone to download your SSE-C encrypted data from S3.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/ServerSideEncryptionCustomerKeys] You must use HTTPS when specifying SSE-C headers on your requests.
=== s3-sse-kms-benefits-over-sse-s3 | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S2
C: SSE-KMS lets AWS KMS manage the keys and adds separate permissions for using the KMS key, plus an audit trail that shows who used the key for which object and when, including failed attempts.

S: scope='per object encrypted with SSE-KMS' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] SSE-KMS lets AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) manage your encryption keys. Using AWS KMS to manage your keys provides several additional benefits. With AWS KMS, there are separate permissions for the use of the KMS key, providing an additional layer of control and protection against unauthorized access to your objects stored in Amazon S3.
E: [www/s3/faqs] AWS KMS provides an audit trail so you can see who used your key to access which object and when, as well as view failed attempts to access data from users without permission to decrypt the data.

=== s3-sse-s3-default-every-bucket | default | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S2
C: Every S3 bucket has encryption configured by default and every new object is encrypted at rest with SSE-S3 unless another type is chosen. To use another type, name it in the PUT request (SSE-KMS, DSSE-KMS or SSE-C) or change the bucket default to SSE-KMS or DSSE-KMS.
Q: Is a new S3 object encrypted at rest if nobody configures encryption, and with which key type?
V: default=server-side encryption with Amazon S3 managed keys (SSE-S3) is the default encryption configuration for every bucket in Amazon S3 || auto=all new objects that are uploaded to an S3 bucket are automatically encrypted at rest || bucket-default-choices=if you want to set a different default encryption configuration in the destination bucket, you can use SSE-KMS or DSSE-KMS || short=SSE-S3
S: scope='per bucket' dom='default' adj=True cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/UsingEncryption] All Amazon S3 buckets have encryption configured by default, and all new objects that are uploaded to an S3 bucket are automatically encrypted at rest. Server-side encryption with Amazon S3 managed keys (SSE-S3) is the default encryption configuration for every bucket in Amazon S3. To use a different type of encryption, you can either specify the type of server-side encryption to use in your S3 `PUT` requests, or you can update the default encryption configuration in the destination bucket.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/UsingEncryption] If you want to specify a different encryption type in your `PUT` requests, you can use server-side encryption with AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) keys (SSE-KMS), dual-layer server-side encryption with AWS KMS keys (DSSE-KMS), or server-side encryption with customer-provided keys (SSE-C). If you want to set a different default encryption configuration in the destination bucket, you can use SSE-KMS or DSSE-KMS.
=== s3-standard-default-class | default | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-numbers | bul=4.1.S8

E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/storage-class-intro] By default, objects in S3 are stored in the S3 Standard storage class, however Amazon S3 offers a range of other storage classes for the objects that you store.

S: scope='S3 Standard-IA storage class' dom='' adj=False cond=''

=== s3-standard-ia-vs-one-zone-ia-use | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=4.1.K10,2.2.K11
C: Use S3 Standard-IA for the primary or only copy of data that cannot be re-created. Use S3 One Zone-IA when you can re-create the data if the Availability Zone fails, for example for replicas made by Cross-Region Replication; it stores data in only one Availability Zone and is not resilient to the loss of that zone.
Q: When is One Zone-IA acceptable instead of Standard-IA?
V: standard-ia=primary or only copy of data that can't be re-created || one-zone=if you can re-create the data if the Availability Zone fails || risk=not resilient to the physical loss of the Availability Zone
S: scope='storage class choice' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/storage-class-intro] **S3 One Zone-IA** (`ONEZONE_IA`) – Amazon S3 stores the object data in only one Availability Zone, which makes it less expensive than S3 Standard-IA. However, the data is not resilient to the physical loss of the Availability Zone resulting from disasters, such as earthquakes and floods. The S3 One Zone-IA storage class is as durable as S3 Standard-IA, but it is less available and less resilient.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/storage-class-intro] **S3 Standard-IA** (`STANDARD_IA`) – Use for your primary or only copy of data that can't be re-created.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/storage-class-intro] **S3 One Zone-IA** (`ONEZONE_IA`) – Use if you can re-create the data if the Availability Zone fails, for object replicas when configuring S3 Cross-Region Replication (CRR).

=== s3-versioning-get-delete-marker-404 | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5
C: After a delete in a versioned bucket, the delete marker is the current version; a plain GET returns a 404 Not Found error, but a GET that names an older version ID still returns that version.
Q: What does a GET return when the current version of an object is a delete marker?
V: v1=404 Not Found || v2=The delete marker becomes the current version
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/versioning-workflows] The delete marker becomes the current version of the object. By default, `GET` requests retrieve the most recently stored version. Performing a `GET Object` request when the current version is a delete marker returns a `404 Not Found` error, as shown in the following figure.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/versioning-workflows] However, you can `GET` a noncurrent version of an object by specifying its version ID.
=== s3-versioning-never-back-to-unversioned | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5
C: Versioning is enabled and suspended at the bucket level. After a bucket is version-enabled it can never return to an unversioned state; it can only be suspended.
Q: Can you turn versioning off on a bucket after enabling it?
V: v1=can never return to an unversioned state || v2=you can *suspend* versioning on that bucket
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/Versioning] You enable and suspend versioning at the bucket level. After you version-enable a bucket, it can never return to an unversioned state. But you can *suspend* versioning on that bucket.
=== s3-versioning-new-version-only-on-put | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5
C: New object versions are created only when a new object is PUT (CopyObject also works through a PUT); changing the tags on an object does not create a new version.
Q: Does changing an object tag create a new version in a versioned bucket?
V: v1=don't create a new version || v2=changing the tags on an object
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/versioning-workflows] New versions of objects are created only when you `PUT` a new object. Be aware that certain actions, such as `CopyObject`, work by implementing a `PUT` operation.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/versioning-workflows] Some actions that modify the current object don't create a new version because they don't `PUT` a new object. This includes actions such as changing the tags on an object.

=== s3-versioning-preserves-on-overwrite-and-delete | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5
C: Once versioning is enabled, S3 keeps the existing object whenever you PUT, POST, COPY or DELETE it. After a DELETE, simple un-versioned requests no longer return the object, but every version is still preserved and can be retrieved or restored, and only the bucket owner can permanently delete a version.
Q: How does versioning protect against accidental overwrites and deletes?
V: preserve=preserves existing objects anytime you perform a PUT, POST, COPY, or DELETE operation on them || after-delete=all versions of that object will continue to be preserved in your Amazon S3 bucket and can be retrieved or restored || owner=only the owner of an Amazon S3 bucket can permanently delete a version
S: scope='per versioning-enabled bucket' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] Once you enable Versioning for a bucket, Amazon S3 preserves existing objects anytime you perform a PUT, POST, COPY, or DELETE operation on them. By default, GET requests will retrieve the most recently written version. Older versions of an overwritten or deleted object can be retrieved by specifying a version in the request.
E: [www/s3/faqs] When a user performs a DELETE operation on an object, subsequent simple (un-versioned) requests will no longer retrieve the object. However, all versions of that object will continue to be preserved in your Amazon S3 bucket and can be retrieved or restored. Only the owner of an Amazon S3 bucket can permanently delete a version.
=== s3-versioning-recovers-unintended-actions | capability | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=1.3.S5,2.2.K11
C: S3 Versioning preserves, retrieves and restores every version of every object in a bucket, so you can recover from unintended user actions and application failures.
Q: What does S3 Versioning protect you against?
V: recover=recover from both unintended user actions and application failures || keeps=every version of every object
S: scope='per bucket' dom='' adj=False cond='versioning enabled on the bucket'
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/DataDurability] You can use versioning to preserve, retrieve, and restore every version of every object that is stored in your Amazon S3 bucket. With versioning, you can easily recover from both unintended user actions and application failures.
=== s3-versioning-rollback-window-with-lifecycle | capability | hard=False | extended | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5,4.1.K7
C: Versioning plus Lifecycle rules can give a rollback window: a rule can archive previous versions to a cheaper class and delete them after a set number of days, or delete noncurrent versions after some days when at least a chosen number of newer versions exist.
Q: How do you build a recycle-bin or rollback window for S3 objects?
V: how=use Amazon S3 Lifecycle rules along with S3 Versioning to implement a rollback window || newer=deleting old (noncurrent) versions of an object after five days and when there are at least two newer versions of the object
S: scope='per versioning-enabled bucket' dom='' adj=True cond=''
E: [www/s3/faqs] You can use Amazon S3 Lifecycle rules along with S3 Versioning to implement a rollback window for your S3 objects.
E: [www/s3/faqs] Additionally, you can save costs by deleting old (noncurrent) versions of an object after five days and when there are at least two newer versions of the object. You can change the number of days or the number of newer versions based on your cost optimization needs.
=== s3-versioning-suspend-keeps-existing-versions | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5
C: Suspending versioning stops new versions from accruing; existing objects and versions in the bucket do not change, only how S3 handles future requests.
Q: What happens to existing object versions when you suspend versioning?
V: v1=existing objects in your bucket do not change || v2=stop accruing new versions
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/VersionSuspendedBehavior] In Amazon S3, you can suspend versioning to stop accruing new versions of the same object in a bucket.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/VersionSuspendedBehavior] When you suspend versioning, existing objects in your bucket do not change. What changes is how Amazon S3 handles objects in future requests.
=== s3-versioning-three-states-all-objects | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S5

=== s3-vpc-endpoint-keeps-traffic-off-internet | capability | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=1.3.K1,1.3.S6
C: A VPC endpoint for S3 is a logical entity inside a VPC that connects only to S3, so traffic does not cross the open internet; bucket policies can restrict access to a given VPC or endpoint, and a VPC without an internet gateway limits data exfiltration.
Q: How do you give private instances S3 access without crossing the internet, and lock the bucket to that path?

S: scope='per VPC endpoint and per bucket policy' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/security-best-practices] A virtual private cloud (VPC) endpoint for Amazon S3 is a logical entity within a VPC that allows connectivity only to Amazon S3. VPC endpoints can help prevent traffic from traversing the open internet.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/security-best-practices] You can control which VPCs or VPC endpoints have access to your S3 buckets by using S3 bucket policies.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/security-best-practices] You can help prevent data exfiltration by using a VPC that does not have an internet gateway.
=== s3-vpc-endpoint-private-connection | behavior | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=1.3.S6,4.1.K1
C: A VPC endpoint for S3 is a logical entity inside a VPC that allows connectivity only to S3 and needs no internet, VPN, NAT instance or Direct Connect path; it changes only how requests are routed, and public S3 endpoints keep working.
Q: What does a VPC endpoint for S3 change, and does it need internet access?
V: private=doesn't require access over the internet || routing=VPC endpoints change only how requests are routed || only-s3=allows connectivity only to Amazon S3
S: scope='per VPC endpoint' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/example-bucket-policies-vpc-endpoint] A VPC endpoint enables you to create a private connection between your VPC and another AWS service. This private connection doesn't require access over the internet, through a virtual private network (VPN) connection, through a NAT instance, or through Direct Connect.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/example-bucket-policies-vpc-endpoint] A VPC endpoint for Amazon S3 is a logical entity within a VPC that allows connectivity only to Amazon S3. The VPC endpoint routes requests to Amazon S3 and routes responses back to the VPC. VPC endpoints change only how requests are routed. Amazon S3 public endpoints and DNS names will continue to work with VPC endpoints.

=== s3-vs-file-storage-shared-mount | capability | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=3.1.K2

It provides simple storage for workloads that need access to shared data across multiple compute instances.

=== s3-war-customer-managed-keys-for-sensitive-data | behavior | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=1.3.S2
C: Well-Architected guidance: AWS-managed and service-controlled KMS keys are used on your behalf by AWS; for sensitive data that needs fine-grained control of the key, use customer managed keys, which you fully control including rotation and key policies.

V: when=sensitive data that requires fine-grained access to the underlying encryption key || control=full control over CMKs, including rotation and access management through the use of key policies

E: [docs/wellarchitected/latest/security-pillar/sec_protect_data_rest_encrypt] Default and service-controlled AWS KMS keys are managed and used on your behalf by AWS. For sensitive data that requires fine-grained access to the underlying encryption key, consider customer managed keys (CMKs). You have full control over CMKs, including rotation and access management through the use of key policies.

=== s3-war-object-lock-even-root-user | capability | hard=False | core | stable | by=mine-21-decisions | bul=1.3.K3

Additionally, [Amazon Glacier Vault Lock](https://docs.aws.amazon.com/amazonglacier/latest/dev/vault-lock.html) offers a similar feature for archives stored in Amazon Glacier.

=== s3-website-sse-kms-needs-cloudfront-oac | restriction | hard=True | core | stable | by=mine-21-behavior | bul=3.1.K2,1.3.S2
C: A static website bucket encrypted with SSE-KMS must be served through a CloudFront distribution, because SSE-KMS does not support anonymous users; the distribution must use origin access control (OAC), since OAI does not support SSE-KMS.
Q: How do you serve a static website from an S3 bucket encrypted with SSE-KMS?
V: v1=SSE-KMS doesn't support anonymous users || v2=you must use OAC instead
S: scope='' dom='' adj=False cond=''
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/WebsiteHosting] If the bucket that you're using to host your static website has been encrypted using server-side encryption with AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) keys (SSE-KMS), you must create an Amazon CloudFront distribution to serve your website because SSE-KMS doesn't support anonymous users.
E: [docs/AmazonS3/latest/userguide/WebsiteHosting] OAI doesn't support SSE-KMS, so you must use OAC instead.
